I remember thinking I could take any custom order that came in and turn it into some showpiece nobody else around here could really touch. It felt like confidence at the time, you know? Maybe more than normal confidence.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you spend much more money than usual or make impulsive purchases?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so, not in any huge way. I mean, with my work there’s always materials and tools and stuff, so sometimes money goes out and I can explain it as business, you know?\n\nBut if you mean like going on shopping sprees or buying things totally out of nowhere, I’m not sure that really fits me. At least I haven’t noticed it as a major thing.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did they ever cause serious problems at work or in your relationships, or lead to you being hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Not hospitalized, no. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was.\n\nBut… yeah, it did cause problems. I missed a big custom delivery, which is really embarrassing to say because that’s not how I usually run my shop. And my records got so messy that I’ve had to stop taking new work for now, just until I can get things straightened out.\n\nMy family’s been checking on me more too. I think they got worried because I wasn’t keeping up with my normal routines the way I usually do. So… yes, I guess it did affect work and home more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever believe things that other people said weren’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like believing things that couldn’t be true, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I had some big ideas and I probably talked like I was more sure of myself than usual, but nobody sat me down and said, “That’s not real,” or anything. At least not that I remember. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did your most noticeable wired or unusually energized period last?\n\nPatient: I guess the most noticeable one was after I got that award at the jewelry show. At the time I kept telling myself I was just excited and motivated, but yeah, about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you talking much more than usual or so fast that other people had trouble interrupting you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. It felt like if I didn’t get it all moving right then, I’d lose the momentum.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, but it was definitely more than my normal pace.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you more easily distracted than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually one of the bigger things I noticed, if I’m being honest.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark I want to fix, then hear something outside or remember another order, and suddenly I’ve lost track of what I was doing. At the time I thought I was just motivated, but… yeah, it was definitely less sleep than I normally need.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at times, I guess I did. Especially after the show, I felt kind of… lit up, like everything was possible and I was bigger than my usual self, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but one minute I’d be in this amazing mood, and then if someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or slow down, I’d snap at them. Not proud of that. But yeah, there was definitely a kind of high feeling there.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I thought I was better than everyone, exactly. But after I won that ribbon, I did feel like I’d finally proved something.\n\nLike… I was on another level, maybe. I remember thinking I could take any custom order that came in and turn it into some showpiece nobody else around here could really touch. It felt like confidence at the time, you know? Maybe more than normal confidence.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you spend much more money than usual or make impulsive purchases?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so, not in any huge way. I mean, with my work there’s always materials and tools and stuff, so sometimes money goes out and I can explain it as business, you know?\n\nBut if you mean like going on shopping sprees or buying things totally out of nowhere, I’m not sure that really fits me. At least I haven’t noticed it as a major thing.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did they ever cause serious problems at work or in your relationships, or lead to you being hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Not hospitalized, no. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was.\n\nBut… yeah, it did cause problems. I missed a big custom delivery, which is really embarrassing to say because that’s not how I usually run my shop. And my records got so messy that I’ve had to stop taking new work for now, just until I can get things straightened out.\n\nMy family’s been checking on me more too. I think they got worried because I wasn’t keeping up with my normal routines the way I usually do. So… yes, I guess it did affect work and home more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever believe things that other people said weren’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like believing things that couldn’t be true, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I had some big ideas and I probably talked like I was more sure of myself than usual, but nobody sat me down and said, “That’s not real,” or anything. At least not that I remember. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did your most noticeable wired or unusually energized period last?\n\nPatient: I guess the most noticeable one was after I got that award at the jewelry show. It didn’t feel that long at first, but looking back… it was probably about two weeks in a row.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot. At the time I kept telling myself I was just excited and motivated, but yeah, about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you talking much more than usual or so fast that other people had trouble interrupting you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably talked more than I normally do, just because I was excited, but I don’t know that people had trouble interrupting me.\n\nNobody really told me I was talking too fast or anything like that. If they thought it, they didn’t say it directly. So… I’d say maybe a little more talkative, but I’m not sure it was that extreme.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it racing exactly. My mind was definitely busy, like I had a lot of ideas and plans popping up, but I don’t know if it was out of control or anything.\n\nMaybe faster than usual, but… I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. Does that count as racing?\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you more driven than usual to start or push through lots of projects or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I was trying to be productive, not reckless or anything, but I did start a lot at once.\n\nI was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers, making plans for a website, and sketching out this whole Appalachian collection, all kind of at the same time. It felt like if I didn’t get it all moving right then, I’d lose the momentum.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, but it was definitely more than my normal pace.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you more easily distracted than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually one of the bigger things I noticed, if I’m being honest.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark I want to fix, then hear something outside or remember another order, and suddenly I’ve lost track of what I was doing. Like I’ll look down and realize I don’t even have the whole work order straight in my head anymore.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely useless or anything, but it’s been harder to stay on one thing than usual. That’s kind of what made me feel like I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Did the heavy, hollow feeling start after that two-week wired period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it was after that kind of wound down. These past couple weeks especially, I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow feeling.\n\nI’m still getting to the shop, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything, but it’s like the shine’s gone out of everything. And… this is kind of embarrassing, but I have cried in the truck before opening up. Just sitting there trying to pull myself together.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I normally like, or at least I try to. It’s not like I suddenly hate jewelry work or anything.\n\nMaybe I don’t get the same kick out of it lately, but I don’t know if that counts as “lost interest.” I might just be tired or in a rut. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you noticed your appetite has changed much from usual?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, my eating’s probably a little irregular just because of work and all that, but I don’t think my appetite is dramatically different or anything.\n\nI’m still eating. I haven’t really been tracking it, though. Is that okay to say if I’m not totally sure?\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been sleeping more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: A little less, I guess. I mean, I’m still trying to go to bed at a normal time, so it’s not like I’m staying up on purpose.\n\nBut lately I’ll get in bed and just kind of toss around, and then I’m up before dawn thinking about that missed delivery. And when I finally drag myself up, I don’t really feel rested.\n\nIs that the kind of sleep change you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly… I don’t want to sound dramatic.

But these past couple weeks, yeah, I’ve been waking up with this heavy, kind of hollow feeling. Like the shine’s gone out of everything, if that makes sense. And… I’m a little embarrassed to say this, but I’ve cried in my truck before going in to open the shop.

So, I guess maybe that’s close to what you mean?

Especially after the show, I felt kind of… lit up, like everything was possible and I was bigger than my usual self, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but one minute I’d be in this amazing mood, and then if someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or slow down, I’d snap at them. Not proud of that. But yeah, there was definitely a kind of high feeling there.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I thought I was better than everyone, exactly. I think they got worried because I wasn’t keeping up with my normal routines the way I usually do. So… yes, I guess it did affect work and home more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever believe things that other people said weren’t true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like believing things that couldn’t be true, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I had some big ideas and I probably talked like I was more sure of myself than usual, but nobody sat me down and said, “That’s not real,” or anything. At least not that I remember. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did your most noticeable wired or unusually energized period last?\n\nPatient: I guess the most noticeable one was after I got that award at the jewelry show. It didn’t feel that long at first, but looking back… it was probably about two weeks in a row.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot. At the time I kept telling myself I was just excited and motivated, but yeah, about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you talking much more than usual or so fast that other people had trouble interrupting you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably talked more than I normally do, just because I was excited, but I don’t know that people had trouble interrupting me.\n\nNobody really told me I was talking too fast or anything like that. If they thought it, they didn’t say it directly. It felt like if I didn’t get it all moving right then, I’d lose the momentum.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, but it was definitely more than my normal pace.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you more easily distracted than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually one of the bigger things I noticed, if I’m being honest.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark I want to fix, then hear something outside or remember another order, and suddenly I’ve lost track of what I was doing.
